                        <path class="sticoncomment" d="M13.6,7.1H6.4c-0.3,0-0.6-0.3-0.6-0.6c0-0.3,0.3-0.6,0.6-0.6l7.2,0c0.3,0,0.6,0.3,0.6,0.6  C14.2,6.8,13.9,7.1,13.6,7.1L13.6,7.1z M13.6,9.4H6.4c-0.3,0-0.6-0.3-0.6-0.6s0.3-0.6,0.6-0.6l7.2,0c0.3,0,0.6,0.3,0.6,0.6  C14.2,9.2,13.9,9.4,13.6,9.4L13.6,9.4z M11.5,11.7H6.4c-0.3,0-0.6-0.3-0.6-0.6c0-0.3,0.3-0.6,0.6-0.6h5.1c0.3,0,0.6,0.3,0.6,0.6  C12.1,11.5,11.8,11.7,11.5,11.7z M15.8,3H4.2C3.5,3,3,3.5,3,4.2V17l2.8-2.3h10c0.6,0,1.2-0.5,1.2-1.2V4.2C17,3.5,16.5,3,15.8,3  L15.8,3z"></path>
                        </svg>
                    </div>
                </li>
                <li class="nav-item">
                    <div class="more-link-wrapper more-link-wrapper-mobile">
                        <span class="more-nav-link">
                            <div class="mobile-nav-icon">
                                <svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" width="25px" height="25px" viewBox="0 0 55 55">

                                    <g class="mobile-nav-home">
                                        <path fill-rule="evenodd" clip-rule="evenodd" fill="#FFFFFF" d="M45.201,12.343c0.378,0.48,0.758,0.925,1.096,1.401    c2.975,4.207,4.543,8.876,4.494,14.044c-0.05,5.452-1.643,10.386-5.186,14.593c-3.484,4.133-7.929,6.73-13.182,7.895    c-6.313,1.398-12.216,0.275-17.695-3.131c-0.441-0.273-0.847-0.6-1.266-0.904c-0.11-0.078-0.208-0.174-0.337-0.287    c0.127-0.141,0.246-0.27,0.366-0.398c0.887-0.949,1.765-1.904,2.663-2.844c0.114-0.119,0.321-0.217,0.485-0.217    c3.658-0.006,7.318,0,10.975,0.008c3.458,0.006,6.913,0.02,10.369,0.02c0.957,0,1.871-0.193,2.62-0.844    c0.797-0.693,1.157-1.596,1.157-2.643c0.001-7.533,0.003-15.067-0.005-22.601c-0.002-0.309,0.088-0.524,0.3-0.743    C43.098,14.598,44.127,13.49,45.201,12.343"></path>

    <h1 class="text-center">IR Trends Q1 2026: Phishing reemerges as top initial access vector, as attacks targeting public administration persist</h1>

    <div class="text-center m-1">
        <div class="post-author">
            <span>By </span>
                <a href="https://blog.talosintelligence.com/author/aliza/">Aliza Johnson</a>
        </div>

        <br/>
        <time class="post-datetime" datetime="April 22, 2026 06:00">
            Wednesday, April 22, 2026 06:00
        </time>

        <div class="m-3">
                            <a href="/category/ctir-trends/" class="category primary-category">
                                Talos IR trends
                            </a>
                                                    <a href="/category/ctir-trends-2/" class="category primary-category">
                                CTIR trends
                            </a>
                                </div>
    </div>

    <section class="post-content-wrapper mt-5">
        <div class="post-content" id="post-content">
            <ul><li>Phishing reemerged as the most observed means of gaining initial access, accounting for over a third of the engagements where&nbsp;initial&nbsp;access could be&nbsp;determined.&nbsp;Phishing&nbsp;has not been the top vector for&nbsp;initial&nbsp;access since Q2 2025.</li><li>Public administration and health&nbsp;care&nbsp;tied&nbsp;as the most targeted industry verticals, each accounting for 24&nbsp;percent&nbsp;of all engagements. This is the third consecutive quarter where public administration has been the most targeted industry&nbsp;vertical.&nbsp;&nbsp;</li><li>Pre-ransomware incidents made up just 18&nbsp;percent&nbsp;of engagements this quarter, and we did not&nbsp;observe&nbsp;any ransomware deployment due to early and swift mitigation from&nbsp;Cisco&nbsp;Talos Incident Response (Talos IR). This is a slight increase from last quarter but&nbsp;overall&nbsp;very low&nbsp;compared to Q1 and Q2 2025, when we&nbsp;observed&nbsp;ransomware in 50&nbsp;percent&nbsp;of engagements.</li></ul><hr><figure class="kg-card kg-embed-card kg-card-hascaption"><iframe width="200" height="113" src="https://www.youtube.com/embed/wppL7JBshK8?feature=oembed" frameborder="0" allow="accelerometer; autoplay; clipboard-write; encrypted-media; gyroscope; picture-in-picture; web-share" referrerpolicy="strict-origin-when-cross-origin" allowfullscreen="" title="The TTP 23: AI Phishing, Fake CAPTCHA &amp; Real-World Cyber Threat Trends (Q1 2026)"></iframe><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Watch this video to see Talos experts break down the trends and offer guidance for defenders.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2 id="ai-tool-leveraged-in-phishing-campaign">AI tool&nbsp;leveraged&nbsp;in phishing campaign&nbsp;</h2><p>Talos IR responded to a campaign that&nbsp;leveraged&nbsp;phishing,&nbsp;the most common means of&nbsp;initial&nbsp;access this quarter, to compromise the most targeted industry vertical this quarter:&nbsp;public administration. Notably, the actors&nbsp;leveraged&nbsp;the&nbsp;Softr AI-based web application development service, marking the first time we have documented the use of a specific AI tool by an adversary in a phishing campaign.&nbsp;Softr&nbsp;was used to generate a credential harvesting page targeting users’ Microsoft Exchange and Outlook Web Access (OWA) accounts.&nbsp;</p><p>State-sponsored and criminal actors have been&nbsp;observed&nbsp;abusing large language models (LLMs) to aid in the development of phishing lures, malicious scripts, and other tasks. DDoS-as-a-service actors have adopted AI algorithms for defense evasion and attack orchestration. While this is the first time we have documented the use of a specific AI tool in a Talos IR incident, we have moderate confidence that malicious actors have used&nbsp;Softr’s&nbsp;AI-powered web application creation platform since at May 2023, based on Cisco Umbrella data and other telemetry, and have done so with increasing frequency to date.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</p><p>This incident&nbsp;demonstrates&nbsp;how AI tools can lower the barrier to entry for less sophisticated actors and/or accelerate the speed of phishing and credential-harvesting campaigns. Using a form template and the “vibe coding” feature, a phishing page like the one used in this attack could be quickly created with a few AI prompts and no code. Phishing pages built with&nbsp;Softr&nbsp;can direct data to a disposable external data store, such as Google Sheets, and send alerts for new captures via email&nbsp;—&nbsp;all without code.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</p><h2 id="crimson-collective-seen-for-the-first-time">Crimson Collective seen for the first time&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</h2><p>Talos IR experienced its first case involving Crimson Collective, a cyber extortion group that appeared in September 2025. This attack highlighted&nbsp;the use of valid accounts for&nbsp;initial&nbsp;access, the second&nbsp;most commonly&nbsp;observed&nbsp;means of&nbsp;initial&nbsp;access this quarter. This attack also notably involved targeting exploit weaknesses, the second-most observed security weakness, accounting for 25&nbsp;percent&nbsp;of all engagements.&nbsp;We attribute this activity to Crimson Collective based on IPs associated with the group that were used to scan the victim's ASA firewalls, as well as an overlap of observed tactics and techniques with publicly reported Crimson Collective attacks.&nbsp;</p><p>The incident began when a GitHub Personal Access Token (PAT) was inadvertently published on a public-facing&nbsp;website, exposing the organization to adversaries for several months. Upon obtaining access, the adversary used&nbsp;TruffleHog, an open-source tool commonly&nbsp;utilized&nbsp;by security professionals, to scan thousands of&nbsp;victim&nbsp;GitHub repositories for&nbsp;additional&nbsp;secrets and sensitive information. This approach allows attackers to perform reconnaissance without triggering suspicion, as they are&nbsp;leveraging&nbsp;standard, legitimate tools. The attacker’s discovery of client secrets through&nbsp;TruffleHog&nbsp;enabled further access to&nbsp;the victim’s&nbsp;Azure cloud storage, where they used Microsoft Graph API calls to authenticate, explore, and exfiltrate data. The abuse of legitimate cloud APIs&nbsp;demonstrates&nbsp;a growing trend where threat actors use native platform functionality to blend into normal user activity, making detection more challenging.&nbsp;</p><p>In addition to exfiltrating data, the adversary&nbsp;attempted&nbsp;to inject malicious code into multiple GitHub repositories. This code was designed to harvest any new secrets committed in the future, sending them to adversary-controlled infrastructure. Though these attempts were&nbsp;largely thwarted&nbsp;by the&nbsp;expiration&nbsp;of targeted secrets and effective security controls, the tactic reflects an emerging trend of supply chain and development environment attacks.&nbsp;&nbsp;</p><h2 id="ransomware-trends">Ransomware trends&nbsp;</h2><h3 id="ransomware-experiences-slight-increase-remains-low-overall">Ransomware experiences slight increase,&nbsp;remains&nbsp;low overall&nbsp;&nbsp;</h3><p>Pre-ransomware incidents made up just 18 percent of engagements this quarter, and we did not&nbsp;observe&nbsp;any ransomware&nbsp;encryption&nbsp;due to early and swift mitigation from Talos IR. This is a slight increase from last quarter, when ransomware and pre-ransomware collectively&nbsp;comprised&nbsp;13 percent of engagements, but&nbsp;overall&nbsp;very low&nbsp;compared to Q1 and Q2 2025, when we&nbsp;observed&nbsp;ransomware in 50 percent of engagements. Attribution is challenging in pre-ransomware events&nbsp;because&nbsp;there are no encryptors or ransom notes, but we assess that&nbsp;Rhysida&nbsp;ransomware and&nbsp;MoneyMessage&nbsp;ransomware accounted for two of the engagements.&nbsp;</p><p>While we did not&nbsp;observe&nbsp;many active and prolific ransomware-as-a-service (RaaS) operations, like&nbsp;Qilin&nbsp;or Akira, this&nbsp;likely does&nbsp;not&nbsp;indicate&nbsp;these major players are decreasing operations, as their data leak sites&nbsp;remain&nbsp;consistently active.&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</p><h3 id="rhysida-ransomware-actors-use-uncommon-backdoor-meowbackconn">Rhysida&nbsp;ransomware actors use&nbsp;uncommon backdoor,&nbsp;Meowbackconn&nbsp;&nbsp;</h3><p>Talos&nbsp;IR&nbsp;responded to a ransomware incident where the adversary&nbsp;attempted&nbsp;to deploy&nbsp;Rhysida&nbsp;ransomware. While the attack was mitigated in the pre-ransomware stage, we attribute this activity with moderate confidence to&nbsp;Rhysidabased on observed infrastructure that is associated with&nbsp;Rhysida&nbsp;activity and the use of&nbsp;Gootloader, which is commonly&nbsp;leveraged&nbsp;in&nbsp;Rhysida&nbsp;attacks during&nbsp;initial&nbsp;access.&nbsp;Notably, the actors deployed proxy-related DLLs (e.g.,&nbsp;“meow_eu.dll”), which we assess were&nbsp;likely related&nbsp;to&nbsp;MeowBackConn, an uncommon backdoor that is&nbsp;closely associated&nbsp;with&nbsp;Gootloader, based on public reporting.&nbsp;</p><p>This attack&nbsp;represents&nbsp;several trends that we&nbsp;observed&nbsp;throughout Talos IR engagements in Q1 2026.&nbsp;The environmental weaknesses that enabled this intrusion — exposed&nbsp;WinRM&nbsp;management ports, over-privileged service accounts, and critical logging gaps — directly echo this quarter’s most prominent security weaknesses, including vulnerable or exposed infrastructure, accounting for 25 percent of engagements. Furthermore, the adversary’s use of&nbsp;Remote Desktop Protocol (RDP)&nbsp;for lateral movement is consistent with RDP being the top technique for lateral movement for the previous two quarters (Q3 and Q4 2025).</p><h3 id="targeting">Targeting</h3><figure class="kg-card kg-image-card"><img src="https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/2026/04/Q1dark_Q4Targets-dark-1-.jpg" class="kg-image" alt="" loading="lazy" width="2000" height="777" srcset="https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w600/2026/04/Q1dark_Q4Targets-dark-1-.jpg 600w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w1000/2026/04/Q1dark_Q4Targets-dark-1-.jpg 1000w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w1600/2026/04/Q1dark_Q4Targets-dark-1-.jpg 1600w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w2400/2026/04/Q1dark_Q4Targets-dark-1-.jpg 2400w" sizes="(min-width: 720px) 720px"></figure><p>Public administration and health&nbsp;care were tied as the most targeted industry verticals. Notably, Q3 2025 marked the first time public administration&nbsp;emerged&nbsp;as the most targeted sector in Talos IR engagements, and it has&nbsp;retained&nbsp;that position since.&nbsp;Organizations within the public administration sector are attractive targets as they are often underfunded and use legacy equipment. These entities may have access to sensitive data as well as a low downtime tolerance, making them attractive to financially motivated and espionage-focused threat groups.</p><h3 id="initial-access">Initial access</h3><figure class="kg-card kg-image-card"><img src="https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/2026/04/Q1dark_Q4InfectionVectors-dark.jpg" class="kg-image" alt="" loading="lazy" width="1875" height="712" srcset="https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w600/2026/04/Q1dark_Q4InfectionVectors-dark.jpg 600w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w1000/2026/04/Q1dark_Q4InfectionVectors-dark.jpg 1000w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w1600/2026/04/Q1dark_Q4InfectionVectors-dark.jpg 1600w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/2026/04/Q1dark_Q4InfectionVectors-dark.jpg 1875w" sizes="(min-width: 720px) 720px"></figure><p>Phishing reemerged as the most observed means of gaining initial access, accounting for over a third of the engagements where&nbsp;initial&nbsp;access could be&nbsp;determined. Phishing was the top&nbsp;initial&nbsp;access vector in the first half of 2025, at which point it was surpassed by exploitation of public-facing applications,&nbsp;likely due to&nbsp;the widespread exploitation of vulnerabilities in on-premises Microsoft SharePoint servers, collectively referred to as&nbsp;ToolShell. Since then, we have&nbsp;observeda steady decrease in the exploitation of public-facing applications as&nbsp;an initial&nbsp;access vector from a high of 62 percent to only 18 percent in Q1 2026. Similarly, in this quarter, valid accounts returned to its pre-ToolShell&nbsp;baseline as the second most&nbsp;observed&nbsp;means of gaining initial access,&nbsp;comprising&nbsp;24 percent of Talos IR engagements. We assess the decline in&nbsp;ToolShell&nbsp;exploitation&nbsp;is&nbsp;likely due to the widespread availability of emergency patches and enhanced security detections, highlighting the importance of&nbsp;timely&nbsp;patching.</p><h2 id="recommendations-for-addressing-top-security-weaknesses">Recommendations for addressing top security weaknesses</h2><figure class="kg-card kg-image-card"><img src="https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/2026/04/Q1dark_Q4SecurityWeakness-dark.jpg" class="kg-image" alt="" loading="lazy" width="1875" height="711" srcset="https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w600/2026/04/Q1dark_Q4SecurityWeakness-dark.jpg 600w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w1000/2026/04/Q1dark_Q4SecurityWeakness-dark.jpg 1000w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/size/w1600/2026/04/Q1dark_Q4SecurityWeakness-dark.jpg 1600w, https://storage.ghost.io/c/af/a0/afa04ee3-414f-4481-8d23-7e7c146f192e/content/images/2026/04/Q1dark_Q4SecurityWeakness-dark.jpg 1875w" sizes="(min-width: 720px) 720px"></figure><h3 id="implement-properly-configured-mfa-and-other-access-control-solutions">Implement properly configured MFA and other access control solutions&nbsp;&nbsp;</h3><p>35 percent of engagements this quarter involved&nbsp;multi-factor authentication (MFA)&nbsp;weaknesses, an increase from last quarter. This includes incidents where threat actors bypassed MFA and where MFA was either missing or only partially enabled, particularly on remote access services. Adversaries were able to bypass MFA by registering new devices to previously compromised accounts, and in one instance, by configuring Outlook clients to connect directly to Exchange servers, circumventing MFA requirements. Addressing these weaknesses, especially by restricting self-service MFA enrollment and enforcing strong, centralized authentication policies, is essential to reducing risk and strengthening organizational resilience.&nbsp;</p><h3 id="conduct-robust-patch-management">Conduct robust patch management&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</h3><p>Vulnerable or exposed infrastructure was another top security weakness accounting for 25 percent of all engagements, a slight decrease from last quarter. This included exploiting a vulnerability (CVE-2025-20393) in the Spam Quarantine feature of Cisco&nbsp;AsyncOS&nbsp;Software for Cisco Secure Email Gateway and Cisco Secure Email and Web Manager, as well as&nbsp;a&nbsp;vulnerability (CVE-2023-20198) in the web UI feature in Cisco IOS XE Software.&nbsp;Talos&nbsp;also&nbsp;observed&nbsp;exposed management ports (such as&nbsp;WinRM&nbsp;open to the internet), which enabled rapid attacker movement and reconnaissance.&nbsp;&nbsp;</p><h3 id="configure-centralized-logging-capabilities-across-the-environment">Configure centralized logging capabilities across the environment&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</h3><p>Finally, 18 percent of engagements this quarter involved organizations with insufficient logging capabilities, which&nbsp;hindered investigative efforts. Understanding the full context and chain of events performed by an adversary on a targeted host is vital not only for remediation but also for enhancing defenses and addressing any system vulnerabilities for the future. To address this issue, Talos IR recommends organizations implement a&nbsp;security&nbsp;information and&nbsp;event&nbsp;management (SIEM) solution for centralized logging. In the event an adversary&nbsp;deletes&nbsp;or modifies logs on the host, the SIEM will&nbsp;contain&nbsp;the original logs to support a forensics investigation.&nbsp;Additionally,&nbsp;Talos IR&nbsp;offers&nbsp;a&nbsp;<a href="https://talosintelligence.com/incident_response/assessment" rel="noreferrer noopener"><u>Log Architecture Assessment</u></a>&nbsp;service, which&nbsp;provides a focused review of an organization’s logs and overall log strategy to&nbsp;identify&nbsp;gaps&nbsp;and&nbsp;offer recommendations that give a complete view of the security environment and strengthen incident response readiness&nbsp;</p><h2 id="mitre-attck-appendix">MITRE ATT&amp;CK appendix&nbsp;</h2><p>The tables&nbsp;below&nbsp;represent&nbsp;the MITRE ATT&amp;CK techniques&nbsp;observed&nbsp;in this quarter’s IR engagements and&nbsp;includes&nbsp;relevant examples and the number of times seen. Given that some techniques can fall under multiple tactics, we grouped them under the most relevant tactic based on the way they were&nbsp;leveraged. Please note&nbsp;that&nbsp;this is not an exhaustive list.&nbsp;</p><p>Key findings from the MITRE ATT&amp;CK framework include:&nbsp;</p><ul><li>Phishing was the top method of&nbsp;initial&nbsp;access, replacing exploitation of public-facing applications which was dominant in the prior two quarters.&nbsp;</li><li>Web-based&nbsp;C2&nbsp;was the most common C2 pattern. Application Layer Protocol over web protocols was&nbsp;observed&nbsp;most often,&nbsp;indicating&nbsp;adversaries&nbsp;frequently&nbsp;blended C2 into normal-looking traffic.&nbsp;</li><li>Lateral movement primarily relied on common remote administration channels. SMB/Windows Admin Shares was the top lateral movement technique, with WMI and RDP also heavily used, suggesting attackers repeatedly&nbsp;leveragedstandard enterprise remote management paths once inside. RDP was the top technique for lateral movement in the prior two quarters.&nbsp;&nbsp;</li><li>Defense evasion&nbsp;frequently&nbsp;focused on weakening visibility and endpoint protections. Impair defenses by disabling/modifying&nbsp;tools appeared multiple times, alongside log/trace reduction behaviors (e.g., clear command history and file deletion),&nbsp;indicating&nbsp;a recurring emphasis on reducing detection and forensic evidence.</li></ul>

    <script>
  function coverageView() {
    const coverageTable = document.getElementById('coverage-block');
    const sidebar = document.getElementById('side-bar');
    const postContent = document.getElementById('post-content');
    const currentWidth = window.innerWidth;  
  
    if (coverageTable && sidebar && postContent) {
      if (currentWidth < 1000) {
        // For viewport less than 1000px, append to post content
        postContent.appendChild(coverageTable);
      } else {
        // For viewport 1000px and wider, check for subscribe button
        const subscribeButton = sidebar.querySelector('.subscribe-button');
        
        if (subscribeButton) {
          // Insert coverage block after the subscribe button
          subscribeButton.insertAdjacentElement('afterend', coverageTable);
        } else {
          // No subscribe button found, prepend to top of sidebar
          sidebar.prepend(coverageTable);
        }
      }
    }
  }
  
  window.addEventListener("resize", coverageView);
  window.addEventListener("load", coverageView);


  (function() {
    Array.from(document.querySelectorAll("a")).filter(a => {
        return a.hostname && a.hostname !== location.hostname
    }).forEach(a => a.setAttribute('target', '_blank'))
})();
</script>
